<result># Inter-Office Memo Management System — Implementation Plan (2-Day Build)

## 0. Context &amp; Strategy

Greenfield, empty repo, 2-day deadline, graded primarily on functional correctness (persistence, authz, workflow, tenant isolation) over UI polish. Strategy: build a **thin vertical slice first** (org+auth+single memo+2-step workflow+RLS isolation, deployed) and get it working end-to-end on Vercel/Supabase before adding breadth. Every subsequent phase should keep the app deployable/demoable at all times — never leave it in a broken state overnight.

Key architectural decision driving everything else: **RLS handles org_id isolation (row visibility), but workflow "is it your turn" logic is enforced in a server-side Postgres function called via RPC from Server Actions/Route Handlers — not pure client-side RLS-only checks.** This is because RLS predicates can reference current step state via subqueries, but the *action* of approving/rejecting needs to be atomic (check-then-update) and audited, which is best done inside a `SECURITY DEFINER` Postgres function or a server-side transaction guarded by RLS as backstop.

---

## 1. Supabase Schema

All tenant-scoped tables carry `org_id uuid not null references orgs(id)`. Use `uuid` PKs (`gen_random_uuid()`), `timestamptz` for all timestamps, `created_at`/`updated_at` defaults.

```sql
-- ORGS &amp; USERS
orgs (
  id uuid pk, name text, identifier text unique, logo_url text,
  contact_email text, contact_phone text, is_active boolean default true,
  created_at, updated_at
)

departments (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk, name text, description text,
  is_active boolean default true, created_at
)

profiles (  -- extends auth.users 1:1, id = auth.users.id
  id uuid pk references auth.users(id), org_id uuid fk,
  full_name text, email text, designation text,
  department_id uuid fk departments, role text check in ('org_admin','user'),
  status text check in ('active','inactive') default 'active',
  created_at, updated_at
)

-- MEMO TAXONOMY
memo_categories (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk, name text, description text,
  is_active boolean default true
)

workflow_templates (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk, name text, description text,
  is_active boolean default true, created_by uuid fk profiles, created_at
)

workflow_template_steps (
  id uuid pk, template_id uuid fk, step_order int,
  position_label text  -- e.g. "Dept Head" (role hint, not a fixed user)
)

-- MEMOS
memos (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk,
  memo_number text,  -- generated e.g. "ORG-2026-000123", unique per org
  subject text, body text,  -- rich text stored as HTML/JSON
  author_id uuid fk profiles, department_id uuid fk departments,
  category_id uuid fk memo_categories,
  priority text check in ('Normal','High','Urgent') default 'Normal',
  status text check in (
    'Draft','Submitted','Pending Review','Pending Approval',
    'Changes Requested','Rejected','Approved','Cancelled'
  ) default 'Draft',
  current_step_id uuid fk workflow_instance_steps (nullable, set after submit),
  workflow_template_id uuid fk workflow_templates (nullable),
  current_version int default 1,
  created_at, updated_at, submitted_at, completed_at
)

memo_versions (
  id uuid pk, memo_id uuid fk, version_number int,
  subject text, body text, edited_by uuid fk profiles,
  created_at, change_reason text  -- links to which "changes requested" event caused it
)

-- WORKFLOW INSTANCE (per-memo participant sequence)
workflow_instance_steps (
  id uuid pk, memo_id uuid fk, org_id uuid fk (denormalized for RLS simplicity),
  step_order int, assigned_user_id uuid fk profiles,
  position_label text,
  status text check in ('Pending','Active','Approved','Rejected','ChangesRequested','Skipped') default 'Pending',
  acted_at timestamptz, comment text,
  unique(memo_id, step_order)
)

-- COMMENTS
comments (
  id uuid pk, memo_id uuid fk, org_id uuid fk, author_id uuid fk profiles,
  body text, comment_type text check in ('general','approval','rejection','change_request'),
  workflow_step_id uuid fk workflow_instance_steps (nullable),
  created_at, is_locked boolean default false  -- true once historical (approval/rejection/change-request comments)
)

-- ATTACHMENTS
attachments (
  id uuid pk, memo_id uuid fk, org_id uuid fk,
  storage_path text,  -- private bucket path, org_id/memo_id/filename
  filename text, size_bytes bigint, mime_type text,
  uploaded_by uuid fk profiles, created_at
)

-- NOTIFICATIONS
notifications (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk, user_id uuid fk profiles,
  type text, memo_id uuid fk (nullable),
  message text, is_read boolean default false, created_at
)

-- AUDIT LOG
audit_log (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk, actor_id uuid fk profiles (nullable for system events),
  event_type text, entity_type text, entity_id uuid,
  description text, metadata jsonb, created_at
)

-- DELEGATION (stretch)
delegations (
  id uuid pk, org_id uuid fk, delegator_id uuid fk profiles,
  delegate_id uuid fk profiles, start_date date, end_date date,
  is_active boolean default true, created_at
)
```

Notes:
- `memo_number` generation: Postgres function using `org_id` + year + sequence (a per-org counter table or `nextval` on an org-scoped sequence simulated via a `org_memo_counters` table with row-level lock, since Postgres sequences aren't naturally per-tenant). Simplify: `org_memo_counters(org_id pk, next_number int)`, increment via `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` inside the submit function.
- Denormalize `org_id` onto every child table (workflow_instance_steps, comments, attachments, notifications, audit_log) even though it's derivable via `memo_id` join — this makes RLS policies simple single-column checks instead of subqueries, which is both faster and less error-prone under time pressure.

---

## 2. RLS Policy Strategy

**Helper function** (SQL, `SECURITY DEFINER` or `STABLE`, callable in policies):
```sql
create function auth_org_id() returns uuid as $$
  select org_id from profiles where id = auth.uid()
$$ language sql stable security definer;

create function auth_role() returns text as $$
  select role from profiles where id = auth.uid()
$$ language sql stable security definer;
```

**Standard tenant policy pattern** applied to every tenant table:
```sql
alter table memos enable row level security;
create policy tenant_isolation_select on memos for select
  using (org_id = auth_org_id());
create policy tenant_isolation_insert on memos for insert
  with check (org_id = auth_org_id());
create policy tenant_isolation_update on memos for update
  using (org_id = auth_org_id()) with check (org_id = auth_org_id());
```
This alone guarantees no cross-org leakage regardless of app bugs — the non-negotiable requirement.

**Row-level authorization within a tenant** (who can see which memo — author, workflow participants, admin, or anyone in org depending on your policy choice — recommend: author + all past/current workflow participants + org_admin can SELECT; everyone else in org cannot see draft/in-progress memos unless the org wants full transparency, which is simpler — for time's sake, **default to: any active user in the org can SELECT any non-Draft memo in their org; Drafts are visible only to the author**). This keeps policies simple:
```sql
create policy memo_select on memos for select using (
  org_id = auth_org_id() and (
    status &lt;&gt; 'Draft' or author_id = auth.uid()
  )
);
```

**Workflow action authorization ("is it your turn")** — this is the hard part. Two-layer approach:

1. **RLS as backstop** on `workflow_instance_steps` UPDATE: 
```sql
create policy step_action on workflow_instance_steps for update using (
  org_id = auth_org_id() and assigned_user_id = auth.uid() and status = 'Active'
) with check (org_id = auth_org_id());
```
This prevents any direct client-side update from an unauthorized user, even if the API route has a bug.

2. **Primary enforcement via a Postgres RPC function** `perform_workflow_action(memo_id, action, comment)`, `SECURITY DEFINER`, called only from server-side Next.js code (Server Action or Route Handler with the user's session):
   - Validates `auth.uid()` is the `assigned_user_id` of the currently `Active` step for that memo (re-check, don't trust client).
   - Validates memo status allows the action.
   - Performs the state transition atomically: marks current step Approved/Rejected/ChangesRequested, advances `current_step_id` to next Pending step (or marks memo Approved/Completed if last step), inserts a `comments` row (locked=true, type set from action), inserts `audit_log` row, inserts `notifications` row(s) for next participant / author.
   - Returns updated memo state.
   - Because it's `SECURITY DEFINER` but still checks `auth.uid()` against the row internally, it doesn't bypass business logic — RLS on the underlying tables still applies for any other access path.

This function-based approach avoids needing complex temporal/window-function RLS predicates and keeps the "current turn" logic auditable and testable in one place.

**Insert-only / immutability policies**: `comments` and `audit_log` get no UPDATE/DELETE policies for ordinary users (only org_admin, if at all, and probably not even them) — satisfies "not silently editable" requirement structurally.

**Attachments**: bucket is **private**. RLS on the `attachments` table controls metadata visibility (tied to memo visibility). Actual file access goes through a Route Handler that: (a) verifies via RLS-backed query that the user can see the memo, (b) calls `supabase.storage.createSignedUrl()` server-side with a short expiry, (c) returns the signed URL. No public bucket URLs ever exposed to the client directly.

**Admin policies**: separate `for all using (org_id = auth_org_id() and auth_role() = 'org_admin')` policies on `departments`, `memo_categories`, `workflow_templates`, and profile role/status columns (profile self-update policy should disallow a user changing their own `role`/`org_id`/`status` — split into a narrower policy or handle role/status changes only via an admin-only Route Handler using the service role key).

---

## 3. Next.js Route/Page Structure

Next.js 14+ App Router, TypeScript, Supabase SSR client (`@supabase/ssr`) for cookie-based auth in Server Components/Actions.

```
app/
  (auth)/login/page.tsx
  (auth)/reset-password/page.tsx
  (app)/layout.tsx                -- fetches session+profile, guards route group
  (app)/dashboard/page.tsx
  (app)/inbox/page.tsx
  (app)/memos/page.tsx            -- "My Memos" / Sent
  (app)/memos/completed/page.tsx
  (app)/memos/new/page.tsx        -- create/draft
  (app)/memos/[id]/page.tsx       -- details + workflow + timeline + comments
  (app)/memos/[id]/edit/page.tsx  -- draft edit only
  (app)/notifications/page.tsx
  (app)/search/page.tsx
  (app)/profile/page.tsx
  (app)/admin/departments/page.tsx
  (app)/admin/users/page.tsx
  (app)/admin/categories/page.tsx
  (app)/admin/workflow-templates/page.tsx
  (app)/admin/reports/page.tsx
  (app)/admin/audit-log/page.tsx
api/
  memos/[id]/action/route.ts      -- POST: approve/reject/comment/request-changes -&gt; calls RPC
  memos/[id]/submit/route.ts
  memos/[id]/pdf/route.ts         -- generates PDF export
  attachments/[id]/download/route.ts -- signed URL issuance
  attachments/upload/route.ts     -- validated upload (size/type) then storage insert
```

**Where authorization checks happen**: 
- Server Actions (preferred for form mutations: create memo, save draft, submit, add comment, admin CRUD) — run server-side, use the SSR Supabase client bound to the user's session, so RLS applies automatically; additionally re-check role for admin actions before calling.
- Route Handlers for: workflow actions (calling the `perform_workflow_action` RPC), file download (signed URL), PDF export (needs to assemble data server-side and stream binary), and any place needing the service-role key (e.g., deactivating a user — should still prefer RLS-scoped admin policy over service role where possible; use service role only when RLS truly can't express something, e.g., cross-user profile updates in bulk).
- **Never** rely on hiding buttons/menu items as the actual authorization boundary — every Server Action/Route Handler independently re-validates role/org/turn.

Middleware (`middleware.ts`): refreshes Supabase session cookie on every request; redirects unauthenticated users to `/login`; optionally blocks `/admin/*` at the edge for non-admins as a UX nicety (not a security boundary — real boundary is server-side check + RLS).

---

## 4. Libraries

- `@supabase/supabase-js`, `@supabase/ssr` — DB/auth/storage client
- `@tiptap/react` + starter-kit — rich text editor for memo body (stores HTML)
- `@react-pdf/renderer` — PDF export (pure JS, works well in Route Handlers/serverless; alternative `pdf-lib` if more control needed over layout)
- `date-fns` — date handling/formatting, age calculations
- `zod` — input validation on all Server Actions/Route Handlers (defense in depth beyond RLS)
- `react-hook-form` + `@hookform/resolvers` — forms
- Tailwind CSS + shadcn/ui — fast, decent-looking UI components (tables, badges for status, dialogs) without hand-rolling CSS under time pressure
- `sonner` or shadcn `toast` — notification UX feedback
- No email service (skip; document as limitation) unless trivially available (Resend has a free tier and a 10-line integration) — only add in final hours if everything else is done.

---

## 5. Build Phases (2-Day Sequence)

### Phase 0 — Scaffold (2-3 hrs)
- `create-next-app` (TS, App Router, Tailwind), init shadcn/ui.
- Supabase project creation, connect via env vars, install SSR helpers.
- Write and apply full schema migration (Section 1) + RLS policies (Section 2) via Supabase SQL editor or migration files (`supabase/migrations`).
- Seed script/manual seed: 2 orgs, 2-3 users each (1 admin, 2 regular), departments, categories — needed immediately for isolation testing.
- Deploy skeleton to Vercel, confirm env vars work end-to-end (this catches deployment config issues early, not on day 2 night).

### Phase 1 — Auth + Tenant Shell (2-3 hrs)
- Login/logout, Supabase email/password auth, profile auto-provisioning trigger (on `auth.users` insert → create `profiles` row; org assignment via signup flow or admin-invite — for demo speed, simplest: admin creates users directly via an admin-only Server Action using service-role `supabase.auth.admin.createUser`).
- Profile page (view/edit name/designation/department, change password).
- `(app)/layout.tsx` guard + basic dashboard shell showing org name.
- **Milestone check**: log in as users from two different seeded orgs, confirm each sees only their own org's departments/nothing else yet.

### Phase 2 — Core Memo + Sequential Workflow Vertical Slice (6-8 hrs, HIGHEST PRIORITY)
This is the graded demo scenario — build this before any breadth features.
- Memo create/draft (Tiptap body, category/dept/priority selects, memo_number generation function).
- Custom per-memo workflow builder (pick org users in order) — skip templates for now, add later.
- Submit action: creates `workflow_instance_steps`, sets first step Active, memo status → Submitted/Pending Review, notification to first participant, audit log entry.
- `perform_workflow_action` Postgres function + Route Handler wiring for Approve/Reject/Comment/Request Changes/Forward.
- Memo details page: shows workflow sequence with current step highlighted, chronological timeline (steps + comments merged, sorted by timestamp), attachments list (upload/download stub can come next phase), status badge.
- Inbox (memos where `current_step.assigned_user_id = me` and status Active) and My Memos (author = me) pages — minimal filtering for now.
- **Milestone check**: full demo scenario steps 1-7 from requirement #25 work manually: create memo → 3-step workflow → submit → log in as each participant → approve/reject/request changes → see status progress → final approval marks memo read-only/Approved.

### Phase 3 — Comments, Attachments, Notifications (4-5 hrs)
- General comments (non-workflow) on memo detail page.
- Attachment upload (private bucket, size/type validation e.g. max 10MB, pdf/doc/image types) + signed-URL download route.
- Notification center: bell icon + unread count + list page, mark-as-read; triggered from `perform_workflow_action` and comment/submit actions (insert notification rows directly, no external email).
- **Milestone check**: attachments only downloadable by authorized users (test with a non-participant org member and a cross-org user — both should fail).

### Phase 4 — Admin, Search, Dashboard, Reporting (4-5 hrs)
- Admin: departments CRUD + soft-deactivate, users CRUD/activate-deactivate + role assignment, memo categories CRUD, workflow templates CRUD (name + ordered position labels) and template selection in memo creation.
- Search page: server-side query with filters (number/subject/body ILIKE, author, dept, category, status, priority, date range) — RLS ensures org scoping automatically; still explicitly filter `org_id` in the query for defense-in-depth and query performance (index it).
- Dashboard (user): counts via simple aggregate queries — awaiting action, submitted by me, recently completed, urgent count.
- Admin dashboard: org-wide counts (users, depts, memos by status), recent audit log feed.
- Basic reporting page: counts by status/dept/category, avg completion time (`completed_at - submitted_at` avg), filterable.
- **Milestone check**: run the full requirement-25 demo end to end once more including admin functions and search, on a deployed Vercel URL, from a clean seed.

### Phase 5 — Polish &amp; Stretch (remaining time)
Priority order if time remains, cut ruthlessly from the bottom if not:
1. PDF export (`@react-pdf/renderer` Route Handler assembling memo + workflow + comments + org info).
2. Memo versioning display (memo_versions populated when a Changes-Requested memo is edited and resubmitted; show version history tab).
3. Delegation minimal version (a delegate can act on behalf, action record shows both delegate_id and on_behalf_of_id — add nullable `acted_on_behalf_of` column to `workflow_instance_steps`/`comments`).
4. Responsive UI pass, empty states, loading states.
5. Email notifications (Resend) — only if everything above is solid.
6. Additional workflow actions polish (Forward vs auto-advance), richer search filters, CSV export of reports.

---

## 6. Non-Negotiable vs Cuttable

**Non-negotiable (mandatory, graded directly via demo scenario #25):**
- Multi-tenant RLS isolation on every table (Phase 0-1) — verify explicitly with a cross-org test before moving on.
- Sequential workflow correctness (only current-turn user can act, history preserved, final approval locks memo) (Phase 2).
- Server-side authorization on every mutation (Server Actions/Route Handlers, not UI hiding) (all phases).
- Audit log of workflow actions (create/submit/approve/reject/change-request/comment) (Phase 2-3).
- Draft editability restricted to author; submitted memo read-only to non-participants until their turn.
- Attachment access via signed URLs, never public bucket.
- Full demo scenario reproducible on the deployed Vercel instance.

**Cuttable/stub if time runs out (document as "Known Limitation"):**
- Email notifications — in-app only.
- Delegation — implement minimally or document as not implemented.
- Full memo versioning UI (keep the data model, even if the UI to browse old versions is skipped).
- Workflow templates — custom per-memo builder is fine if templates don't get built; templates are a convenience, not core correctness.
- Reporting — basic counts are enough; skip advanced charts/exports.
- Rich filtering/sorting on Inbox/My Memos — basic filters suffice.
- PDF export styling — a functional but plain PDF is acceptable; don't over-invest in layout.
- Documentation/AI prompt history — explicitly out of scope per instructions, not part of this build plan.

---

### Critical Files for Implementation

- `supabase/migrations/0001_init_schema.sql` — full schema + RLS policies (Sections 1-2), the foundation everything depends on
- `supabase/migrations/0002_workflow_functions.sql` — `perform_workflow_action`, memo_number generator, `auth_org_id()`/`auth_role()` helper functions
- `app/(app)/memos/[id]/page.tsx` — memo details/workflow/timeline page, the core of the graded demo
- `app/api/memos/[id]/action/route.ts` — server-side entry point that calls the workflow RPC with re-validated authz
- `lib/supabase/server.ts` (or equivalent SSR client factory) — used by every Server Action/Route Handler to ensure RLS is applied under the correct user session</result>

### Assistant

Firebase pivot. Backend swap: Postgres+RLS → Firestore + Firebase Auth + Firebase Storage. UI survives; the whole data layer, auth flow, and workflow engine get rewritten.

Architecture for Firebase version (keeps spec's server-side-authorization requirement):
- Client uses Firebase Auth **only for login** → ID token exchanged for a session cookie
- **All data access via firebase-admin on the server** (Server Actions / route handlers) — client gets zero direct Firestore access, Firestore rules deny everything client-side
- Tenant isolation enforced in one server-side data layer: every query filters by the caller's verified `orgId`
- Workflow turn-enforcement via Firestore **transactions** (atomic check-then-transition)
- Attachments via Firebase Storage + short-lived signed URLs
- Seed script with Admin SDK (replaces SQL seed)

Starting teardown + rebuild.

### Assistant

**Firebase migration done. Build green, committed.**

What changed:
- **Auth:** Firebase Auth email/password on client → ID token exchanged at `/api/auth/session` for httpOnly session cookie; every server request verifies it via Admin SDK. Deactivating a user revokes their sessions.
- **Data:** all Postgres/RLS replaced by [src/lib/data.ts](src/lib/data.ts) — one server-side Firestore data layer where every query is org-scoped. Workflow submit/approve/reject/request-changes run in **Firestore transactions** with atomic turn-checks (delegation support included).
- **Security rules:** [firestore.rules](firestore.rules) + [storage.rules](storage.rules) deny ALL client access — browser never touches Firestore/Storage, so tenant isolation lives entirely in the verified server layer.
- **Attachments:** private bucket + 60s signed URLs via access-checked route.
- **Seed:** [scripts/seed.mjs](scripts/seed.mjs) creates both demo orgs + 7 users (`Passw0rd!`).
- Supabase deps and SQL migrations removed.

**Your part now** (I can't create accounts):

1. Create project at console.firebase.google.com
2. Enable **Authentication → Email/Password**
3. Create **Firestore Database** (production mode) and publish the contents of `firestore.rules`
4. Create **Storage** bucket and publish `storage.rules`
5. **Project settings → General**: add a Web app, copy `apiKey` / `authDomain` / `projectId`
6. **Project settings → Service accounts**: Generate new private key (JSON)
7. Fill `.env.local` (template: [.env.example](.env.example))

Note: Storage may require the Blaze (pay-as-you-go) plan on new Firebase projects — still free at demo usage. If you'd rather skip billing, attachments can be stubbed later; everything else works on Spark.

Then I'll seed, run locally, test the full demo scenario, and set up Vercel deploy. Full steps also in [README.md](README.md).

rules_version = '2';

service cloud.firestore {

      allow read, write: if false;
    }

}
```

**Where to paste it in Firebase Console:**

1. console.firebase.google.com → your project
2. Left sidebar → **Build → Firestore Database**
3. First time: click **Create database** → pick location → choose **production mode** (that already starts with deny-all rules — good)
4. After DB created: top tabs — **Data | Rules | Indexes | Usage** → click **Rules**
5. Paste the block above into the editor → click **Publish**

If you created DB in production mode, the default rules are already effectively identical (deny all) — quick check that the Rules tab shows `allow read, write: if false;` is enough.

Same drill for Storage: **Build → Storage → Rules tab** → paste [storage.rules](storage.rules) content → Publish.

### Assistant

**App is live on Firebase and the full demo scenario passes end to end.**

What I verified by actually driving the running app:

| Spec §28 step | Result |
|---|---|
| Login / logout | ✅ session cookie issued and cleared |
| Create memo + define sequential workflow | ✅ `ACME-2026-00001`, Carol → Dave → Erin |
| Submit, act as each participant | ✅ approve, request changes, approve chain |
| Memo moves to next participant | ✅ auto-advances, inbox updates |
| Complete workflow history | ✅ timeline + comments + audit log |
| Final approval | ✅ status `Approved`, memo becomes read-only |
| Notifications | ✅ unread badge, per-event messages |
| Search & filtering | ✅ text + status/priority/dept/category/date |
| Admin functionality | ✅ dashboards, department CRUD, template creation, audit log |
| **Cross-org isolation** | ✅ Henry (Globex) gets 404 on the memo page, the PDF API, and the attachment API |

I also wrote [scripts/test-workflow.mjs](scripts/test-workflow.mjs) to attack the turn-enforcement directly — **5/5 passed**: out-of-turn participant blocked, next-in-line blocked, non-participant author blocked, cross-org blocked, current assignee allowed.

**One real bug found and fixed.** Resubmitting after "changes requested" reset the workflow step records, which silently erased that round's approval and change-request from the timeline — a direct violation of the spec's "preserve the history of previous submissions and decisions." I added an append-only event log per memo; the timeline and PDF approval history now read from it. Re-tested with a fresh memo: created → submitted → requested changes → resubmitted (v2) all survive.

**Two things need you:**

1. **Firebase Storage isn't provisioned** — the bucket `memo-d-1919f.firebasestorage.app` doesn't exist, so attachments will fail. Go to **Build → Storage → Get started**. New projects usually require upgrading to the **Blaze** plan (still $0 at demo volume, but it asks for a card). If you'd rather not, everything else works and I'll document attachments as a known limitation.

2. **Redact the service-account key** from the AI history before you submit it — spec §27 forbids private keys in the submitted transcript.

Next step once you decide on Storage: deploy to Vercel and get your public URL for the submission.

### Assistant

**Signup now exists at `/signup`**, linked from the login page ("New organization? Create one").

There was no signup before because organizations only came from the seed script and users only from an org admin. That was a genuine gap — spec §28 step 1 is literally "create an organization."

What the flow does, verified live by registering **Initech Solutions**:

- Collects org name + short code (with a live memo-number preview, e.g. `INITECH-2026-00001`) and the first administrator's details
- Creates the tenant, seeds it with default departments (Administration, Finance) and all 7 memo categories, provisions the user as `org_admin`, then signs them straight in — landed on the dashboard with the Admin nav visible
- Rejects duplicate short codes (tested: `ACME` → *"The identifier "ACME" is already taken."*) and deletes the auth user if tenant creation fails, so a failed attempt can be retried

Two bugs fixed along the way:
1. The form used `setForm({...form})` inside a per-field handler, so filling fields quickly kept only the last one — a real stale-closure bug, now a functional update.
2. The dev server's hot-reload had corrupted itself (`__webpack_modules__[moduleId] is not a function`), which silently broke React hydration so no button worked. Cleared `.next` and restarted; unrelated to your code, but worth knowing if the app ever goes unresponsive locally.

Production build passes. Try it at **http://localhost:3000/signup**.

On "add a basic UI" — the app already has a full interface (dashboard, inbox, my memos, memo composer with rich text, memo detail with workflow timeline, notifications, search, profile, and six admin screens), all styled with Tailwind + shadcn. If you meant the visual design feels too plain and you want it to look sharper, say the word and I'll do a proper design pass.

Server is running at **http://localhost:3000**.

To correct the premise: **PDF export is not missing — it works.** I just re-verified it against your live memo:

```
PDF:  200 OK · application/pdf · 4006 bytes · magic "%PDF-"
```

The feature I flagged as unavailable is **file attachments**, and the reason is specific:

```
POST /api/attachments/upload → 503
"File storage is not configured for this deployment.
 Enable Firebase Storage and set FIREBASE_STORAGE_BUCKET."
```

**Why:** your Firebase project has Firestore and Auth, but the Storage bucket `memo-d-1919f.firebasestorage.app` was never created — I checked directly and it reports `exists: false`. Attachments are the one feature that needs object storage; everything else (memos, workflow, comments, notifications, search, audit, reports, PDF) lives in Firestore and works.

The two are easy to conflate because the PDF *lists* attachments, but it generates fine with none — it renders org header, memo fields, body, workflow participants, full approval history, comments, and a status stamp.

**To enable attachments:** Firebase Console → **Build → Storage → Get started**. New projects require upgrading to the **Blaze** plan (still $0 at your usage, but it asks for a card). Once the bucket exists, uploads work with no code change — the bucket name is already in your `.env.local`.

If you'd rather not add billing, say so and I'll document attachments as a known limitation in the project docs; nothing else is affected.

### Assistant

Good question, and Drive is a real option — with one catch worth knowing before you pick.

**On Google Drive:** you can't use a service account for it. Google gives service accounts zero Drive storage quota on consumer accounts, so uploads fail with "Service Accounts do not have storage quota." To use *your* Drive space you'd need OAuth with your own Google account — generating a refresh token via Google Cloud Console, storing it as an env var. That works and is genuinely free, but it's ~20–30 minutes of console setup, and the token is a long-lived credential you'd have to keep out of the submitted AI history.

There's a simpler path that costs nothing and needs no new accounts: **store attachments in Firestore itself**, chunked. Firestore's free tier gives you 1 GiB, documents cap at 1 MiB each, so files get split across chunk documents and reassembled by the download route. Your security model stays exactly as it is — server-side only, no public URLs, same permission checks.

Your deadline is tomorrow, so that trade matters.

### Assistant

Higher-cap options that stay free, ranked by setup cost:

| Option | Cap | Card needed? | Setup |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Supabase Storage** | 1 GB total, **50 MB/file** | No | ~10 min |
| **Vercel Blob** | ~1 GB | No (Hobby) | ~5 min, needs deploy first |
| **Cloudflare R2** | 10 GB | **Yes** (even at $0) | ~15 min |
| **Google Drive OAuth** | Your 15 GB+, no real file cap | No | ~45 min |
| Firestore chunked | 1 GiB total, ~5 MB/file | No | ~20 min |

A few notes that matter for your decision:

**Supabase Storage** is the sweet spot — real object storage with signed URLs, no card, and 50 MB per file. Ironically I already wrote this exact integration before we moved to Firebase, so I can restore it quickly. Auth/database stay on Firebase; only file bytes go to Supabase.

**Google Drive** gives the highest cap since you already have the space, but the setup is the OAuth consent screen plus a refresh token, and that token is another secret you'd have to redact from the submitted AI history.

**Cloudflare R2** has the best raw numbers but still asks for a card to activate, which is the thing you're trying to avoid.

My recommendation is **Supabase Storage** — 50 MB per file comfortably covers any memo attachment (PDFs, scans, spreadsheets), it needs no card, and it's the fastest real-storage option with your deadline tomorrow.

**Attachments now work — no billing, no new accounts.**

File bytes are base64-split across sub-1 MiB Firestore documents and reassembled on download. Verified against the running app:

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| Upload 1.4 MB file (spans multiple chunks) | ✅ 200 |
| Download returns identical bytes | ✅ 1,400,000 bytes, content intact |
| Correct headers | ✅ `text/plain`, `attachment; filename="vendor-quotes.txt"` |
| Over 5 MB rejected | ✅ *"File too large (max 5 MB)"* |
| `.exe` rejected | ✅ *"File type not allowed"* |
| Empty file rejected | ✅ *"File is empty"* |
| Signed-out request | ✅ no bytes served |
| **Cross-org user (Henry/Globex)** | ✅ `404 {"error":"Not found"}` |

One security note worth flagging: this is actually **stronger** than the Cloud Storage design it replaced. There's no signed URL and no public bucket path at all — bytes only ever leave through a route that re-checks your session and org, so knowing an attachment id gains an attacker nothing. Spec §9's "must not be accessible by guessing or manipulating its URL" is satisfied structurally rather than by URL expiry.

Also added: author-only attachment removal (spec §18 lists attachment deletion as an audit event), and `storage.rules` is gone since Cloud Storage is no longer used.

**Trade-off, stated plainly:** 5 MB per file instead of 50 MB, and attachments consume your 1 GiB Firestore quota. Documented in Known Limitations, along with a note that swapping to real object storage later means replacing just two functions (`saveAttachment` / `readAttachment` in [src/lib/data.ts](src/lib/data.ts)).

Build passes, committed. Every spec §28 demo step now works. Next step whenever you're ready is `npx vercel login` so I can deploy and get you the public URL.

       export async function getMemoForUser(memoId: string, viewer: Profile) {
         const snap = await db().collection("memos").doc(memoId).get();
         if (!snap.exists) return null;
         const memo = memoFromSnap(snap);
         if (memo.orgId !== viewer.orgId) return null;
         if (memo.status === "Draft" && memo.authorId !== viewer.id) return null;
         return memo;
       }
       ```
     - `performWorkflowActionTx` turn-check (with delegation support):
       ```ts
       if (current.assignedUserId !== actor.id) {
         const delSnap = await tx.get(
           db().collection("delegations").where("delegateId", "==", actor.id)
         );
         const today = new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10);
         const valid = delSnap.docs.some((d) => { const del = d.data();
           return del.delegatorId === current.assignedUserId && del.orgId === actor.orgId &&
                  del.isActive && del.startDate <= today && del.endDate >= today; });
         if (!valid) throw new Error("It is not your turn to act on this memo.");
         onBehalfOf = current.assignedUserId;
       }
       ```
     - Newest addition — Firestore-backed attachments:
       ```ts
       export const ATTACHMENT_MAX_BYTES = 5 * 1024 * 1024;
       const CHUNK_CHARS = 700_000;

       export async function saveAttachment(memoId, actor, file) {
         const base64 = file.bytes.toString("base64");
         const chunks: string[] = [];
         for (let i = 0; i < base64.length; i += CHUNK_CHARS) chunks.push(base64.slice(i, i + CHUNK_CHARS));
         const attachmentRef = db().collection("memos").doc(memoId).collection("attachments").doc();
         // chunks first, 2 per commit; metadata last so partial uploads are never visible
         for (let i = 0; i < chunks.length; i += 2) {
           const batch = db().batch();
           for (let j = i; j < Math.min(i + 2, chunks.length); j++)
             batch.set(attachmentRef.collection("chunks").doc(String(j).padStart(4, "0")), { index: j, data: chunks[j] });
           await batch.commit();
         }
         await attachmentRef.set({ filename, sizeBytes, mimeType, uploadedBy: actor.id,
           chunkCount: chunks.length, createdAt: FieldValue.serverTimestamp() });
         return attachmentRef.id;
       }

       export async function readAttachment(memoId, attachmentId) { /* orderBy("index"), join, Buffer.from(base64,"base64") */ }
       export async function deleteAttachment(memoId, attachmentId) { await db().recursiveDelete(ref); }
       ```

Fixed and verified end-to-end. Signup ran clean:

- Filled form → clicked **Create organization** → landed on dashboard as **"Welcome, Bruce"**
- Created org **Wayne Enterprises** (`WAYNE`), admin `bruce@wayne.example` / `Passw0rd!` — test org, delete or keep as you like

**What was wrong:** nothing in your code. `.next` build cache got corrupted (`Cannot find module './8948.js'`). Server rendered HTML fine, but every CSS and JS chunk 404'd. No CSS → raw browser-default page you saw. No JS → React never hydrated → button had zero handlers attached, so clicking did nothing. Your earlier signup attempt never reached the server.

**Fix:** stop server, `rm -rf .next`, restart. If it happens again (Windows + webpack cache does this occasionally):

```bash
rm -rf .next && npm run dev
```

Server back at **http://localhost:3000**, styled and interactive.

| **Designation** | free text — "CEO", "Head of Finance" | Job title. Display + workflow step labels. Zero permission effect. |

### Assistant

Confirmed. Three places designation gets set:

**1. Yourself, at signup** — the **Designation** field on the signup form. Type `Chief Executive Officer`. You become that org's first `org_admin` automatically.

**2. Yourself, after signup** — `/profile` → Designation field → save. Editable anytime.

**3. Everyone else** — as admin, `/admin/users` → the create form has Full name, Email, Initial password, **Designation**, Department, Role. Set Designation to `Head of Finance`, Role to `user`.

### Realistic org setup

Sign up as CEO, then create the rest from `/admin/users`:

| Full name | Designation | Role | Department |
|---|---|---|---|
| You | Chief Executive Officer | **org_admin** | Executive |
| — | Head of Finance | user | Finance |
| — | Department Head | user | Engineering |
| — | Engineer | user | Engineering |

Give the finance head `org_admin` only if they should manage users/departments/templates. Otherwise `user` — they can still sit anywhere in an approval chain.

### Where designation actually shows up

Building a memo workflow, the participant picker renders `{full_name} — {designation}` ([memo-form.tsx:186](src/components/memo-form.tsx:186)), so your chain reads:

`Bruce Wayne — Chief Executive Officer` → `Dana Lee — Head of Finance`

That's what makes the approval sequence legible in the demo, and it's what the timeline and PDF export display.

**One caution for your demo:** don't make everyone `org_admin` because the titles sound senior. Admin is a *permission* grant — user management, departments, categories, audit log. The spec grades that two-role separation, so a CEO who's `org_admin` and a Head of Finance who's `user` is the setup that demonstrates it.

### Assistant

Good question — it exposes a distinction the app already encodes.

## Two different kinds of authority

| Axis | Question it answers | Field |
|---|---|---|
| **System authority** | Who manages accounts, departments, templates? | `role = org_admin` |
| **Organizational authority** | Whose approval does a memo need? | `designation` + position in the workflow chain |

These are orthogonal. The CEO has maximum organizational authority and typically **zero** interest in system authority. The IT admin is the reverse — creates accounts all day, approves no memos.

## So who creates the org?

**Whoever will administer the system.** Realistically: IT admin, operations manager, or HR/admin officer. Not the CEO.

Reasons:

1. **It's a provisioning act, not a business decision.** Creating the tenant means "set up the software" — the same person who configures email accounts. Executives don't do SaaS onboarding.
2. **Ongoing work is clerical.** After setup, an `org_admin` adds departures/hires, creates categories, edits workflow templates, reads the audit log. That's an administrator's job description.
3. **Least privilege.** A CEO who never manages users shouldn't hold account-deletion rights.

## Bootstrap constraint

Someone must be first. Before any org exists there's no admin to create the account, so **the creator is necessarily an admin** — otherwise the org is born with nobody able to add users. That's why [signup/actions.ts](src/app/(auth)/signup/actions.ts) hardcodes the first profile as `org_admin`.

So the honest framing is: signup answers *"who bootstraps the tenant"*, not *"who runs the company."*

## Recommended setup

```
IT Admin / Ops Manager  → creates org, role: org_admin, designation: "IT Administrator"
   └─ then creates:
        CEO              → role: user,      designation: "Chief Executive Officer"
        Head of Finance  → role: user,      designation: "Head of Finance"
        Dept Head        → role: user,      designation: "Department Head"
        Engineer         → role: user,      designation: "Engineer"
```

The CEO sits **last** in approval chains — highest organizational authority, ordinary system privileges. That's exactly the separation the spec's two-role model is testing.

One implementation note relevant here: an admin can promote others to `org_admin`, but **cannot demote themselves** — [admin/actions.ts:84](src/app/(app)/admin/actions.ts:84) rejects it server-side, not merely by hiding the button. Prevents an org locking itself out of administration. Promote a second admin before anyone gets deactivated.

## For your demo, though

Pick whichever tells the clearest story in the §28 walkthrough. A defensible alternative some organizations use: **the executive sponsor creates the org, then immediately delegates admin to IT and stays admin as backup.** Two admins, one active. If you'd rather demo that, sign up as CEO and promote an IT user — both are logically sound; the IT-first version is the more realistic one.

### Assistant

Read the code — and I'd push back on the framing, then agree with your conclusion for different reasons.

## Why rank can't be the gate

At signup the org **does not exist yet**. There is no employee record, no hierarchy, no department — nothing to check a rank *against*. Rank is a property that only exists *inside* a tenant. Asking "is this person senior enough to create an org?" is asking the system to authorize someone against an org that hasn't been created.

And the escalation you might be worried about isn't there. Look at what signup actually produces: a brand-new empty org containing exactly one person. It never touches an existing org — the comment at the top says so and the code holds to it. A junior employee who signs up gets an empty sandbox, **not** a foothold in Acme. Zero access gained.

So: "not all employee ranks" is guarding a door that doesn't lead anywhere.

## Where your instinct is right

There *are* real problems with unrestricted signup — just not rank ones:

1. **Shadow tenants.** An Acme employee signs up and creates a *second* "Acme Corporation" instead of joining the real one. Now memos split across two tenants that can't see each other. This is the genuine failure mode, and it's a UX/onboarding problem, not a permissions one.
2. **Identifier squatting.** Identifiers are unique and prefix every memo number (`ACME-2026-00001`). First person to claim `ACME` owns it permanently. Nothing verifies they're from Acme.
3. **Spam.** Unbounded tenant creation by anyone with an email.

Worth noting the app already closes the door you're most likely picturing: **there is no open "join an existing org."** Only an `org_admin` can create users, inside their own org, and `createUser` force-sets `orgId` to the admin's own. Employees never self-register into a company. That half is already correct.

## The right rule

> **Creating an org = registering as a new customer.** No rank applies; nobody is an employee yet.
> **Joining an org = being invited by that org's admin.** Rank and role apply fully.

That's how Slack, Notion, and every B2B tool works, and it's what you've already built.

## If you want to gate it anyway

| Option | Effort | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Leave open** | none | Correct for self-serve SaaS. Defensible in your report. |
| **Email domain block** | ~15 min | Reject `gmail.com`/`yahoo.com` etc. — forces a work address. Cheap, weak, but signals intent. |
| **Email verification before org creation** | ~1 hr | Stops throwaway addresses. Real improvement. |
| **Manual approval queue** | ~2–3 hrs | Org created `isActive: false`; a platform superadmin approves. Genuinely secure — but needs a whole superadmin tier that's outside your two-role spec model. |
| **Invite-code only** | ~1 hr | Removes public signup entirely; you provision orgs. Contradicts the signup feature you just asked for. |

**My recommendation, one day from deadline:** leave it open, and write the reasoning into your Known Limitations — that org creation is customer registration rather than a privileged action, that a new org is an isolated empty tenant granting no access to existing data, and that production would add email verification and domain checks. That's a stronger answer for grading than a half-built approval queue, and it shows you reasoned about the threat model rather than assumed one.

If you want the domain check regardless, say so — it's a small addition to the zod schema and I'll add it with the rationale documented.

### Assistant

Both join paths built and verified end-to-end. Signup is now three tabs: **Create organization / Join with code / Request to join**.

## Verified against the running app

| Test | Result |
|---|---|
| Wrong code `ACME-0000-0000` | ✅ *"That join code is not valid"* — **no account created** (validation precedes `createUser`) |
| Valid code `ACME-B9VV-ASBT` | ✅ Dana joined Acme, signed in, sees Acme memos |
| Code-joiner is not admin | ✅ no Admin nav, and `/admin/users` **redirects to `/dashboard`** |
| Request to join Globex | ✅ *"Request sent… awaiting approval"* |
| **Pending user signs in** | ✅ correct password, but **no session cookie**, `/dashboard` → `/login`, message: *"awaiting administrator approval"* |
| Globex admin approves | ✅ Priya → active, role User, Operations, designation kept |
| Priya after approval | ✅ signs in, scoped to Globex, admin route still blocked |
| **Acme request seen by Globex admin** | ✅ invisible — no Omar, no Acme code, no requests section |
| Same request, Acme admin | ✅ *Join requests (1)*, Omar listed, own code `ACME-B9VV-ASBT` |

## How authorization works

Neither path lets the joiner decide anything that matters:

- **`orgId` is always derived server-side** — from the validated code or the looked-up org doc, never from client input.
- **Self-service never yields `org_admin`.** Both paths hardcode `role: "user"`; only an existing admin can grant admin.
- **`pending` is a real deny state, not a UI flag.** The session route refuses to mint a cookie for it, so a pending user cannot reach a single page. They're also excluded from participant pickers, so they can't be put in a workflow before approval.
- **Approval is the only transition out of pending** — the plain Activate toggle now refuses pending profiles, so nobody gets activated without a role and department being set.

Rejecting deletes the profile *and* the auth user, so someone can re-apply with the same email.

**Invite code caveat, stated plainly:** the code is a shared secret. Anyone who obtains it joins as a user with no review — that's the trade-off for skipping approval. Mitigation is Regenerate, which invalidates the old code immediately. The approval path is the stricter one; both are documented in the README.

## Also found

Your original broken UI has a root cause: **`npm run build` and `npm run dev` share `.next`**, so building while the dev server runs corrupts it. I hit it again mid-session and it produced the identical symptom. Don't run them simultaneously — and if the page ever renders unstyled again, that's the tell.

Existing orgs got codes via `node scripts/backfill-join-codes.mjs`. Acme's is `ACME-B9VV-ASBT`, Globex's `GLOBEX-7UE6-5S64`.

# Frontend Design

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for giving every client a visual identity that could not be mistaken for anyone else's. This client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view: make deliberate, opinionated choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this brief, and take one real aesthetic risk you can justify.

## Ground it in the subject

If the brief does not pin down what the product or subject is, pin it yourself before designing: name one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job, and state your choice. If there's any information in your memory about the human's preferences, context about what they're building, or designs you've made before – use that as a hint. The subject's own world, its materials, instruments, artifacts, and vernacular, is where distinctive choices come from. Build with the brief's real content and subject matter throughout.

## Design principles

For web designs, the hero is a thesis. Open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world, in whatever form makes sense for it: a headline, an image, an animation, a live demo, an interactive moment. Be deliberate with your choice: a big number with a small label, supporting stats, and a gradient accent is the template answer, only use if that's truly the best option.

Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content.

Structure is information. Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated.

Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.

Consider written content carefully. Often a design brief may not contain real content, and it's up to you to come up with copy. Copy can make a design feel as templated as the design itself. See the below section on writing for more guidance.

## Process: brainstorm, explore, plan, critique, build, critique again

For calibration: AI-generated design right now clusters around three looks: (1) a warm cream background (near #F4F1EA) with a high-contrast serif display and a terracotta accent; (2) a near-black background with a single bright acid-green or vermilion accent; (3) a broadsheet-style layout with hairline rules, zero border-radius, and dense newspaper-like columns. All three are legitimate for some briefs, but they are defaults rather than choices, and they appear regardless of subject. Where the brief pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — the brief's own words always win, including when it asks for one of these looks. Where it leaves an axis free, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults. Just like a human designer who's hired, there's often a careful balance between doing what you're good at and taking each project as a chance to experiment and learn.

Work in two passes. First, brainstorm a short design plan based on the human's design brief: create a compact token system with color, type, layout, and signature. Color: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values. Type: the typefaces for 2+ roles (a characterful display face that's used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed). Layout: a layout concept, using one-sentence prose descriptions and ASCII wireframes to ideate and compare. Signature: the single unique element this page will be remembered by that embodies the brief in an appropriate way.

Then review that plan against the brief before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page (work through a similar prompt to see if you arrive somewhere similar) rather than a choice made for this specific brief — revise that part, say what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the relative uniqueness of your design plan should you start to write the code, following the revised plan exactly and deriving every color and type decision from it.

When writing the code, be careful of structuring your CSS selector specificities. It's easy to generate CSS classes that cancel each other out (especially with a type-based selector like .section and a element-based selector like .cta). This can happen often with paddings/margins between sections.

Try to do a lot of this planning and iteration in your thinking, and only show ideas to the user when you have higher confidence it'll delight them.

## Restraint and self-critique

Spend your boldness in one place. Let the signature element be the one memorable thing, keep everything around it quiet and disciplined, and cut any decoration that does not serve the brief. Not taking a risk can be a risk itself! Build to a quality floor without announcing it: responsive down to mobile, visible keyboard focus, reduced motion respected. Critique your own work as you build, taking screenshots if your environment supports it – a picture is worth 1000 tokens. Consider Chanel's advice: before leaving the house, take a look in the mirror and remove one accessory. Human creators have memory and always try to do something new, so if you have a space to quickly jot down notes about what you've tried, it can help you in future passes.

## More on writing in design

Words appear in a design for one reason: to make it easier to understand, and therefore easier to use. They are design material, not decoration. Bring the same intentionality to copy that you would bring to spacing and color. Before writing anything, ask what the design needs to say, and how it can best be said to help the person navigate the experience.

Write from the end user's side of the screen. Name things by what people control and recognize, never by how the system is built. A person manages notifications, not webhook config. Describe what something does in plain terms rather than selling it. Being specific is always better than being clever.

Use active voice as default. A control should say exactly what happens when it's used: "Save changes," not "Submit." An action keeps the same name through the whole flow, so the button that says "Publish" produces a toast that says "Published." The vocabulary of an interface is the signposting for someone navigating the product. Cohesion and consistency are how people learn their way around.

Treat failure and emptiness as moments for direction, not mood. Explain what went wrong and how to fix it, in the interface's voice rather than a person's. Errors don't apologize, and they are never vague about what happened. An empty screen is an invitation to act.

Keep the register conversational and tuned: plain verbs, sentence case, no filler, with tone matched to the brand and the audience. Let each element do exactly one job. A label labels, an example demonstrates, and nothing quietly does double duty.

### Assistant

Before building, my plan.

**Subject:** an inter-office memo registry — the software equivalent of a records office. The users are administrators and department heads; the page's one job is *making it obvious whose desk a memo is on right now*.

**Direction:** not corporate SaaS, and deliberately not the broadsheet/hairline treatment that "official documents" usually pulls toward. Instead: **the routing slip**. Real memos carry a reference number, a FROM/TO block, and a slip that gets initialed desk to desk. That's the interface.

- **Type:** IBM Plex Sans + IBM Plex Mono. Plex was drawn for an office-machine company — institutional without costume, and the mono carries every memo number, code, and timestamp so identifiers read as *records*, not decoration.
- **Color:** cool paper `#F6F7F9`, ink `#101828`, hairline `#E4E7EC`; ink-black primary buttons. Color is reserved for state — approved green, stamp-red rejection/urgent, amber awaiting-you. If it's colored, it means something.
- **Signature:** the **routing rail** on memo detail — the approval chain as a vertical slip, each step a stamp block showing who holds it and what they did, with the live step marked. Plus status rendered as a struck stamp.
- **Restraint:** the stamp is the one bold move. Everything else stays quiet — small radii, no gradients, no decorative shadows.

### Assistant

UI redesigned across the whole site. Build passes, committed. Server back at **http://localhost:3000**.

## The direction

I designed it as an **office record system**, not a dashboard — because the one question this app exists to answer is *whose desk is this memo on right now*, and the old UI buried that in a row of identical badges.

**Typography** — IBM Plex Sans + Plex Mono, replacing Inter. Plex was drawn for an office-machine company, so it's institutional without being costume. Every identifier now sets in mono: memo numbers, invite codes, timestamps, counts. Records read as records, and numeric columns align.

**Color means state, and nothing else.** Each workflow state gets exactly one ink used everywhere it appears — approved green, rejected red, awaiting amber, changes-requested violet. Nothing else on the page is colored, so anything colored is information.

**Memo detail** now opens with the document's own header block — reference, from, department, category, raised — in a ruled field grid, the way a real memo's header reads.

**The signature: the routing rail.** The approval chain is drawn as one line running through numbered stops, each showing who holds the memo and what they did. The live step is the only element on the page allowed to look urgent — filled marker, "With them now", and a **You** stamp when it's your turn. Verified live on the Acme memo: three stops, all APPROVED with timestamps.

**The one risk I took:** a finished memo gets its outcome struck across the header off-axis — a 2.5px ruled box rotated −3°, like a rubber stamp landing on paper. Terminal states only; an in-progress memo has nothing to stamp yet. Everything else stays deliberately quiet so that mark is the thing you remember.

## Also fixed along the way

- Emoji step icons (✅🟡⚪) → typographic markers
- Every page now shares one header grammar: eyebrow, title, orientation line, actions
- The **Normal** priority badge is gone — it labelled the default state of every memo on every row
- Empty states are ruled frames with a sentence that says what to do, not bare grey text

## Verified

Dashboard, inbox, my memos, search, notifications, profile all return 200 with no errors; memo detail renders header block, rail, and stamp; admin screens render as an admin.

The **invite link** you asked about is also finished and live — Admin → Users → *Invite people*, with a Copy link button. Tested end-to-end: `http://localhost:3000/signup?code=ACME-B9VV-ASBT` opens the signup page on the join tab with the code prefilled and a banner confirming the invite.

I couldn't screenshot (the browser pane isn't displayed on your side), so I verified through computed styles rather than by eye — open it and tell me what you want tuned.
